 Translate the following sentence into English. Mīlitibus ad urbem revenientibus, imperātor sacrificium offerēbat. ANSWER: WHEN THE SOLDIERS WERE RETURNING TO THE CITY, THE EMPEROR/GENERAL OFFERED A SACRIFICE.  BONUS: Translate this sentence into English. Imperātōre urbem ingressūrō, cīvēs laetissimī conclāmābant. ANSWER: WHEN THE EMPEROR/GENERAL WAS ABOUT TO ENTER THE CITY, THE VERY HAPPY CITIZENS WERE SHOUTING. 
 What Latin verb with what meaning lies at the root of nuisance, noxious, pernicious, and innocent? ANSWER: NOCĒRE “TO HARM”  BONUS: What Latin verb with what meaning lies at the root of cinch, cincture, and succinct? ANSWER: CINGERE “TO SURROUND/GIRD”

 Some Latin rhetorical devices are still used in modern pop music. When Sabrina Carpenter’s Please Please Please says “don’t prove I’m right,” “don’t bring me to tears,” and “don’t embarrass me,” what rhetorical device is shown by the repetition of “don’t?” ANSWER: ANAPHORA (DO NOT ACCEPT TRICOLON)  BONUS: What rhetorical device is shown both by Ennius writing “at tuba terribili sonitu taratantara dixit” and Sabrina Carpenter writing “my tongue goes numb, sounds like bleh-blah-blee?” ANSWER: ONOMATOPOEIA

ANSWER: RESULT BONUS: What use of the subjunctive is found in the following sentence? Gladiātor, quem omnēs laudent, dōna semper accipit. ANSWER: RELATIVE CLAUSE OF CHARACTERISTIC

ANSWER:PURPOSE GERUNDIVE  BONUS: What grammatical use of the verb traduco can be found in this sentence: Traducito hostes! ANSWER:FUTURE IMPERATIVE (SINGULAR)  
What independent use of the subjunctive can be found this sentence: Utinam dei nobis istam auctoritatem darent! ANSWER:OPTATIVE  BONUS: Translate that sentence into English. (WOULD THAT) (O THAT) THE GODS WERE GIVING SUCH  ANSWER:AUTHORITY TO US!  BONUS: Translate the sentence if the verb were ‘dent’ instead of ‘darent’  ANSWER:(WOULD THAT) (O THAT) THE GODS (MAY) GIVE SUCH AUTHORITY TO US!  
Distinguish between ordior and orior. ANSWER:ORDIOR = BEGIN; ORIOR = RISE  BONUS: Give the principal parts of both these verbs. ANSWER:ORDIOR, ORDIRI, ORSUS; ORIOR, ORIRI, ORTUS  BONUS: A derivative of one of these verbs means “what you should do before you get started on something”. What is this English word? ANSWER:ORIENT

What is the meaning of the correlative pair cum…tum? ANSWER:BOTH…AND / NOT ONLY…BUT ALSO / WHEN…THEN  BONUS: What is the meaning of the correlative pair sive…sive? ANSWER:WHETHER…OR BONUS: What is the meaning of the correlative pair quā…quā? ANSWER:ON THE ONE HAND…ON THE OTHER HAND

Listen to the following passage, which I will read twice, and answer in English the question that follows. Aurelia tandem fortunam datur confrontāre inimicam suam. Ad puellam inimicam ambulāvit et in vultō inimicae dīxit, “audī, puella. Vox tua similis unguibus in tabulā calcis est! Semper mē nocēs cum iam vulneror. Das mihi sensum mē nullam esse. Sed nescis quid nescias.” Hīs dictīs, Aurelia, nolēns puellam pulsāre, sē vertit, et  discēssit. QUESTION: How does Aurelia describe the other girl’s voice? ANSWER:LIKE NAILS ON A CHALKBOARD  BONUS: What are two things that the enemy does to Aurelia? ANSWER:HURTS HER WHEN SHE IS ALREADY HURT AND MAKES HER FEEL LIKE NOTHING BONUS: After Aurelia said what she had to say, what did she do and why? ANSWER:SHE DID NOT WANT TO PUNCH/HIT HER ENEMY/THE OTHER GIRL, SO SHE TURNED AND LEFT

ANSWER:YOU CAN’T ALWAYS GET WHAT YOU WANT  BONUS: Many modern songs can actually be traced back to ancient Roman melodies – what would we have called in English the hit Roman song “dum cithara mea leniter lacrimat”? ANSWER:WHILE MY GUITAR GENTLY WEEPS  BONUS: What song, probably by the same band, would the Romans have simply called “Fiat”? ANSWER:LET IT BE

Sed si me roges, dicam Linguam latinam esse meliorem quod lingua latina est lingua certaminis” The Question: Cur est lingua Latina melior quam lingua Graeca? What Latin phrase is a plot device whereby a seemingly inextricable problem is suddenly and abruptly solved with the contrived and unexpected intervention of some new event, character, ability, or object? ANSWER:DEUS EX MACHINA  BONUS: What Latin phrase is figurative equivalent to the English saying “blood from a stone”? ANSWER:LANA AB ASINO  BONUS: What Latin phrase refers to a slip of the pen? This is the . Congratulations on making it this far. Since we’re at AMSA, let’s start off by testing your math vocabulary in addition to your knowledge of English words derived from Latin. What English derivative of the Latin word limax, meaning “snail”, is a special type of a polar curve that is defined to be a roulette formed when a circle rolls around the outside of a circle of equal radius? ANSWER:LIMAÇON (SAY LEE-MAH-SOHN)  BONUS: What English derivative of the Latin word for “heart” is a special case of a limaçon in which the polar curve has a cusp? ANSWER:CARDIOID  BONUS: Give the Latin root and its meaning from which the word that is both the formal name of the infinity sign and the name of another common polar curve is derived. ANSWER:LĒMNISCĀTUS, MEANING “DECORATED WITH RIBBONS” (THE WORD ITSELF IS “LEMNISCATE.”) 
Differentiate in meaning between the verbs “miror” and “minor.” ANSWER:MIROR MEANS “TO WONDER OR BE SURPRISED, AMAZED” AND MINOR MEANS “TO THREATEN”  BONUS: Differentiate in meaning between the verbs “neco” and “necto.” ANSWER:NECO MEANS “TO KILL” AND NECTO MEANS “TO INTERTWINE OR WEAVE”  BONUS: Differentiate in meaning between “praeda” and praedo.” ANSWER:PRAEDA MEANS “LOOT, BOOTY, PLUNDER” AND PRAEDO MEANS “PIRATE”

Bob has 3 apples, and Jane has 6 apples. Jane says, “Dabo patri omnia mala mea.” Bob, upset that Jane will upstage him yet again, replies, “Oh yeah? Well, dabo patri omnia mala mea et tua!” Are there any grammatical errors in Bob’s Latin? ANSWER:NO  BONUS: Jane, always ready to show off her own Latin knowledge, says “Ha-ha! Sum melior te non solum numerante malis sed etiam dicente linguam Latinam.” What grammatical error in Latin, if any, has Jane made now? ANSWER:SHE USES THE ABL. PL. OF “MALUM” INSTEAD OF THE ACC. PL.  BONUS: Their father, hearing the argument, comes outside and shouts “Do you kids ever get along?” If Bob translates this phrase into Latin in his head, he comes up with “Cooperatis, liberi, umquam?” What grammatical error in Latin, if any, has Bob made? ANSWER:HE FORGOT TO ADD THE ENCLITIC ‘NE’ TO THE VERB ‘COOPERATIS’

 Although the ablative is more often used, sometimes the agent of a passive verb is expressed with the dative. What construction, which employs the future passive participle, usually exhibits this variation? ANSWER: GERUNDIVE OF OBLIGATION / PASSIVE PERIPHRASTIC  BONUS: However, like many things in Latin, there are exceptions. When is the agent of a passive periphrastic construction expressed with the ablative? ANSWER: WHEN THE VERB NORMALLY TAKES A NON-ACCUSATIVE OBJECT [E.G. WORDS LIKE CRĒDŌ, FRUOR, ETC. / STUDENT MAY CITE “PUFFV” VERBS] 
 What verbal form has only an accusative singular and ablative singular form, with each of these forms having restricted use? ANSWER: SUPINE  BONUS: When used in the ablative case, the supine always has whatuse? ANSWER: RESPECT

 Consider these two sentences: “Sunt quī rīdent” and “Sunt quī rīdeant.” What semantic difference does the presence of the subjunctive in the second sentence indicate? ANSWER: THE ANTECEDENT OF THE RELATIVE PRONOUN IS NOT A SPECIFIC PERSON OR GROUP, BUT A GENERIC GROUP DEFINED BY THE RELATIVE CLAUSE (SHOWING CHARACTERISTIC)  BONUS: What is the name for this use of the subjunctive? ANSWER: RELATIVE CLAUSE OF CHARACTERISTIC Page 17  BONUS: A relative clause with a subjunctive verb is also used to indicate what? ANSWER: PURPOSE / SUBORDINATE CLAUSE IN INDIRECT DISCOURSE

ANSWER: MOLLIŌ – SOFTEN / TAME / WEAKEN / MITIGATE, POLIŌ – POLISH / REFINE / SMOOTH Page 18  BONUS: Differentiate in meaning between lībō and lībrō. ANSWER: LĪBŌ – NIBBLE / SIP / POUR (AS AN OFFERING), LĪBRŌ – WEIGH / LEVEL / BALANCE

ANSWER: WHEN/ALTHOUGH DIDO WAS COMING/CAME TO AENEAS’S BEDROOM/ROOM BONUS: How would you translate the indirect question in the following sentence: Dīdō Aenēān rogāvit cūr eam tam miserābilem fēcisset.? ANSWER: WHY HE HAD MADE HER SO MISERABLE

 Translate the infinitive into English based on its use in the following Latin sentence: sciō Latīnam optimam linguam esse. ANSWER: “IS” BONUS: Now do the same for this sentence: scīvimus Latīnam optimam linguam esse. ANSWER: “WAS”

 Using the 3rd declension noun mare, maris, n. sea, caerulēus, a, um meaning blue, and altus, -a, -um meaning “deep,” give in the genitive plural the phrase “the deep, blue sea.” ANSWER: ALTŌRUM/CAVŌRUM/PROFUNDŌRUM CAERULĒŌRUM MARIUM BONUS: How would you say the same phrase in the ablative singular?  Distinguish in meaning between taceō and faveō. ANSWER: BE SILENT vs. CHERISH / SUPPORT / FAVOR BONUS: Speaking of the second conjugation, distinguish in meaning between caveō and exerceō. ANSWER: BEWARE vs. TRAIN / PRACTICE
 An ut clause with a subjunctive verb is one way to express purpose in Latin. Name another way to express purpose in Latin. (N.B. Moderator, do not read all of the possible correct answers—see bonus below.) ANSWER: ONE OF THE FOLLOWING: AD + GERUND/GERUNDIVE; CAUSĀ/GRATIĀ + (GENITIVE FORM OF THE ) GERUND/GERUNDIVE; RELATIVE CLAUSE OF PURPOSE; (ACCUSATIVE) SUPINE BONUS: Name one other way of expressing purpose in Latin. ANSWER: ONE OF THE FOLLOWING NOT PREVIOUSLY NAMED
 What derivative of the Latin noun meaning “body” is an English adjective that means “portly” or “overweight”?  What literary device, excluding any form of alliteration, is shown in the following passage, which I will read as prose: hīc illius arma, hīc currus fuit; hoc rēgnum dea gentibus esse... iam tum tenditque fovetque? ANSWER: ANAPHORA BONUS: What literary device, excluding any form of alliteration, is shown in the following passage, which I will read as prose: ac velut in somnīs, oculōs ubī languida pressit nocte quiēs, nēquīquam avidōs extendere cursūs velle vidēmur et in mediīs cōnātibus aegrī succīdimus? ANSWER: SIMILE

Cum Cicerō apud Damasippum cēnāret, ille mediocre vīnum posuit et dīxit, “Bibe hoc vīnum; est quadrāgintā annōrum!” Cicero respondit, “Vītam bonam ēgit!” Question: What did Damasippus offer Cicero?  What two elements does the gerundive of obligation construction, also known as the obligatory gerundive or the passive periphrastic, require? ANSWER: GERUNDIVE/FUTURE PASSIVE PARTICIPLE + FORM OF SUM BONUS: What case is used to express agency in a passive periphrastic construction? ANSWER: THE DATIVE CASE BONUS: How would you translate the following impersonal gerundive of passive periphrastic construction: Rōmae nōbīs dormiendum erat? ANSWER: IT HAD TO BE SLEPT IN/AT ROME BY US / WE HAD TO SLEEP IN ROME (N.B. Do not accept “WE MUST...” due to the tense of ERAT)

 What is the best translation for the ablative phrase in the following sentence: Ulixēs tōtum annum apud Circam morātus est et quattuor mēnsibus domum advēnit.? ANSWER: ULYSSES/ODYSSEUS WAS A SOURCE OF RUIN/WAS FOR A RUIN/WAS A DESTRUCTION FOR HIS ADVERSARIES/RIVALS, WITH THE RESULT THAT/THAT (THEY) ALL WERE KILLED/HAVE BEEN KILLED TO A MAN/TO ONE MAN
 Distinguish in meaning between lūpus and lepus. ANSWER: WOLF vs. RABBIT BONUS: What is another Latin noun meaning “rabbit”? ANSWER: CUNĪCULUS BONUS: Distinguish in meaning between the animals glīs and grūs. ANSWER: (DOR)MOUSE vs. CRANE
 In the sentence: These days Orpheus only sleeps for four hours every night. How would you say “for four hours” in Latin? ANSWER: QUATTUOR HŌRĀS BONUS: How would you say in Latin “so that his father may return home” using an ut purpose clause? ANSWER: UT PATER (EIUS) DOMUM REDEAT/REGREDIATUR BONUS: How would you say the following sentence in Latin: “Quintus thought that he was being saved by the pirates.”? ANSWER: QUINTUS CŌGITĀVIT/PUTĀVIT/CREDIDIT SĒ/EUM Ā PĪRĀTĪS SERVĀRĪ
